into the corresponding matrix multiplication rule we proceed as follows

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{n,\alpha k}^{<}=\mathbb{G}^{<}_{nm\sigma}\cdot\mathbb{G}^{<}_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}G^{R}&G^{<}\\
0&G^{A}\end{bmatrix}_{nm\sigma}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}G_{0}^{R}&G_{0}^{<}\\
0&G_{0}^{A}\end{bmatrix}_{\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}

(A.16)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}G_{nm\sigma}^{R}G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{R}&G_{nm\sigma}^{R}G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{<}+G_{nm\sigma}^{<}G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{A}\\
0&G_{nm\sigma}^{A}G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{A}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{bmatrix}G^{R}&G^{<}\\
0&G^{A}\end{bmatrix}_{n,\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}\penalty\ ,

where in the second line, the top right element represents the
lesser function analytical continuation
on the real-time axis of the product
G^{<}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})G^{R}_{0}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})
on
\mathcal{C}_{\tau}
contour as given in eq.
A.13
. As we expected, it is the same result of Eq.
A.14
obtained by direct integration on the Keldysh contour: the matrix representation incorporates the contour calculation on the modified branches. Hence, from the matrix multiplication
\mathbb{G}^{<}\cdot\mathbb{G}_{0}^{<}
we take the top right quantity as the lesser element of the matrix and we plug it in directly as integrand of corresponding real-time integration:

\displaystyle G^{<}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\tau}}G^{<}G_{0}^{R}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad G^{<}(t,t^{\prime})=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{t}}\big(G^{R}G_{0}^{<}+G^{<}G_{0}^{A}\big)\penalty\ .

(A.17)

The matrix representation of contour-ordered Green’s function as derived by Langreth is clearly a more practical tool to derive equations in nonequilibrium condition. For completeness, we summarize the Langreth rules between Keldysh and real-time contour for the case of two terms multiplication

C(\tau,\tau^{\prime})=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{\tau}}AB\Rightarrow\begin{cases}C^{<,>}(t,t^{\prime})=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{t}}\big(A^{R}B^{<,>}+A^{<,>}B^{A}\big)\penalty\ ,\\
C^{R,A}(t,t^{\prime})=\int_{\mathcal{C}_{t}}A^{R,A}B^{R,A}\penalty\ ,\end{cases}

(A.18)

and multiplication for more terms follows similarly.

In conclusion, we have transformed the mixed lesser complex-time variable
G^{<}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})
Green’s function derived in Eq.
A.13
into the mixed lesser real-time variable
G^{<}(t-t^{\prime})
as computed in Eq.
A.14
. This discussion completes the correspondence between equilibrium and nonequilibrium forms. As next step we are ready to insert these analytical expressions into the steady state current in Eq.
A.7
.

Levelwidth function

We introduce the expression for the mixed lesser equilibrium
G^{<}(t-t^{\prime})
derived in Eq.
A.14
into the current in Eq.
A.7

\hat{I}_{L}(t)=2e\mathit{Re}\sum_{nm\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}\Big[V_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}V^{\star}_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dt_{1}\Big(G_{nm\sigma}^{R}(t-t_{1})G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{<}(t_{1}-t^{\prime})+G_{nm\sigma}^{<}(t-t_{1})G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{A}(t_{1}-t^{\prime})\Big)\Big]\penalty\ \

(A.19)

where we have restored the overall parameter summation for the clarity in the derivation.

We are interested in deriving the
steady state time-independent current
, where correlators depend on time differences only. In the retarded and advanced Green’s functions, using the
\mp i\theta(\pm t\mp t^{\prime})
factor in their definition in Eq.
2.6
, we select the non-vanishing time difference. However, in case of lesser Green’s functions, there is no time-ordering symbol in their definition see in Eqs.
2.6
,
2.15
. Hence, we can twist as we need its real-time argument.

We start manipulating the integrand in the current equation by introducing the exact time-independent Green’s functions for isolated leads with noninteracting contacts, namely

\displaystyle G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{<}(t-t^{\prime})=if^{\mu_{\alpha}}(\omega-\mu_{\alpha})e^{-i\omega(t-t^{\prime})}

(A.20)

\displaystyle G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{R/A}(t-t^{\prime})=\mp\theta(\pm t\mp t^{\prime})e^{-i\omega(t-t^{\prime})}\penalty\ ,

where
f^{\mu_{\alpha}}
is the Fermi-Dirac distribution of the initial
\alpha-
lead population uncoupled from the central region given in Eq.
3.10
. In this context, we conclude
f^{\mu_{\alpha}}
is always indicating an equilibrium electronic distribution that is set at distant past, when neither interactions nor tunnelling events occur.

By using these definition for noninteracting in Eq.
A.22
and interacting
G^{<},G^{R}
propagators, we transform the integration variable in
\int d(t-t_{1})
. Then, we shuffle terms in the integrand such that we can assemble the definition of a Fourier transform to energy domain namely

\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d(t-t_{1})G(t-t_{1})e^{-i\omega(t-t_{1})}\equiv G(\omega)\penalty\ ,

(A.21)

since in steady state time-independent current the energy is tunable quantity. The corresponding energy domain expressions after Fourier transform read

\displaystyle G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{<}(\omega)=\int d(t-t^{\prime})G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{<}(t-t^{\prime})e^{i\omega(t-t^{\prime})}=if^{\mu_{\alpha}}(\omega-\mu_{\alpha})2\pi\mathcal{A}_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}(\omega)\penalty\ ,

(A.22)

\displaystyle G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{>}(\omega)=-i(1-f^{\mu_{\alpha}}(\omega-\mu_{\alpha}))2\pi\mathcal{A}_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}(\omega)\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{R/A}(\omega)=\int d(t-t^{\prime})G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{R/A}e^{i\omega(t-t^{\prime})}=\frac{1}{\omega\pm i\eta-\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}}\penalty\ ,

where the equation for
G_{0}^{>}
is just obtained by using the general identity
G^{<}(\omega)=e^{-\beta\omega}G^{>}(\omega)
and the
G^{</>}
equations are the noninteracting version of the fluctuation-dissipation theorem in Eq.
3.11
. For completeness, we also write the noninteracting self-energies

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{<}(\omega)=V_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}V^{\star}_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{<}(\omega)=if^{\mu_{\alpha}}(\omega-\mu_{\alpha})2\Gamma^{\alpha}(\omega)\penalty\ ,

(A.23)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{>}(\omega)=V_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}V^{\star}_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{>}(\omega)=-i(1-f^{\mu_{\alpha}}(\omega-\mu_{\alpha}))2\Gamma^{\alpha}(\omega)\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{R/A}(\omega)=V_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}V^{\star}_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{R/A}(\omega)=\mathit{Re}\Delta_{\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{R}(\omega)\mp\frac{i}{2}\mathit{Im}\Delta_{\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{R}(\omega)\penalty\ ,

where in the lesser and greater equations we derived the second equality using the Fluctuation-Dissipation theorem in Eq.
3.11
and
-\mathit{Im}\Delta_{\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{R}(\omega)\equiv\mathrm{\Gamma}^{\alpha}(\omega)
as we defined in Eq.
3.13
for general
structured
leads such that the Keldysh identity in Eq.
2.16
is fulfilled.

The final current expression is obviously only function of energy, we convert the discrete summation over momentum
\mathbf{k}
into a integral of the density of states
\rho_{0,\alpha\sigma}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})
with respect to
\alpha
-lead

\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\longrightarrow\int\frac{d\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}}{2\pi}\rho_{0,\alpha\sigma}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})\penalty\ .

(A.24)

As final step, we introduce the time-independent
levelwidth function
, that is the matrix form in
n,m
indices of the gamma function seen in Eq.
3.13

[\mathbb{\Gamma}^{\alpha}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})]_{nm}=\pi\mathbf{V}_{\alpha\mathbf{k}n}\mathbf{V}^{\star}_{\alpha\mathbf{k}m}\rho_{0,\alpha\sigma}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})

(A.25)

where the expression satisfies
\forall\penalty\ n,m
central-region degrees with
\rho_{0,\alpha\sigma}
is the noninteracting density of states function with respect to tunnelling in
\alpha
-bath and
\mathbb{V}_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}
is the matrix hybridization strength between
\alpha
-bath and the central interacting region. The gamma expression in Eq.
A.25
is fully dependent on the leads properties and their coupling with the central region. We note also the relation between the single-particle energy
\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}
and momentum dependence in the hybridization is given by
V_{\alpha\mathbf{k}n}=V_{\alpha n}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})=V_{\alpha n}u_{0k}
with
u_{0k}
expansion in momentum basis.

Collecting all these results, we derive the
steady state time-independent current with respect to the left lead

\hat{I}_{L}=ie\sum_{nm\sigma}\int\frac{d\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}}{2\pi}\Big[Tr\Big([\Gamma^{L}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})]_{nm}f^{\mu_{L}}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})(G^{R}_{nm\sigma}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})-G^{A}_{nm\sigma}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}))\Big)+Tr\Big([\Gamma^{L}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})]_{nm}G^{<}_{nm\sigma}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})\Big)\Big]\penalty\ ,

(A.26)

that is the exact expression for interacting mesoscopic transport and it is given with respect to its matrix elements
n,m
. In Eq.
A.26
some crucial properties of the current emerge:
(i)
it is a function of energy,
(ii)
it is determined by local correlations i.e. impurity Green’s functions defined in Eq.
A.9
and
(iii)
it contains indication of the initial thermal equilibrium population in the leads through the Fermi distribution
f^{\mu_{\alpha}}
.

We are only left with deriving the full form of the Meir-Wingreen equation by adding also the contribution to the electronic flow from the right lead.

Time-independent steady state current

Recalling the current conservation property satisfied by our model in steady-state, by performing the same steps presented in this Appendix on the current now flowing from the right lead, we obtain an identical result to Eq.
A.26
. We can then symmetrize the total current composed by these two current equations
\hat{I}_{L},\hat{I}_{R}
calculated separately:

\hat{I}=\frac{\hat{I}_{L}+\hat{I}_{L}}{2}=\frac{\hat{I}_{L}-\hat{I}_{R}}{2}

(A.27)

and we combine the current flow contribution from the left and right leads. The outcome yields to the
dc-
current, time-independent in steady state that is the
Meir-Wingreen formula

\displaystyle\hat{I}=\frac{ie}{2}\sum_{nm\sigma}\int\frac{d\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}}{2\pi}Tr

\displaystyle\bigg[\Big(f^{\mu_{L}}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})[\Gamma^{L}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})]_{nm}-f^{\mu_{R}}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})[\Gamma^{R}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})]_{nm}\Big)\Big(G_{nm\sigma}^{R}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})-G_{nm\sigma}^{A}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})\Big)+

(A.28)

\displaystyle+\Big([\Gamma^{L}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})]_{nm}-[\Gamma^{R}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})]_{nm}\Big)G_{nm\sigma}^{<}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})\bigg]

as it has been derived in
[
105
]
, bring to a completion our derivation. We note the gamma function in the final expression are still evaluated over the matrix components in Eq.
A.25
and all the propagators are locally calculated for the central region.

Meir-Wingreen formula under equilibrium

It is interesting to discuss explicitly the Meir-Wingreen current formula under equilibrium condition.

The Fermi distributions of leads are the same i.e.
f^{\mu_{L}}=f^{\mu_{R}}\equiv f^{Eq}
, provided
\mu_{L}=\mu_{R}\equiv\mu
, hence it reduces to the equilibrium expression in Eq.
2.19
. It follows, the Meir-Wingreen formula under equilibrium condition

\hat{I}=\frac{ie}{2}\sum_{nm\sigma}\int\frac{d\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}}{2\pi}Tr\bigg[f^{Eq}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})[\Gamma^{Eq}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})]_{nm}\Big(G_{nm\sigma}^{R}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})-G_{nm\sigma}^{A}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})\Big)+[\Gamma^{Eq}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})]_{nm}G_{nm\sigma}^{<}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})\bigg]\penalty\ ,

(A.29)

where
G^{<}_{nm\sigma}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})=if^{Eq}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})2\pi\mathcal{A}_{dd\sigma}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})
with the spectral function calculated for local impurity Green’s function as defined in Eq.
3.8
.

Thus, we conclude at equilibrium condition the Meir-Wingreen formula is identically zero, as lack of unequal electronic distribution in the leads
\mu_{L}\neq\mu_{R}
prevents any net current flow through the system.

Meir-Wingreen current formula in time-dependent models

The nonequilibrium procedure to derive steady-state current for time-independent electrical conductance comprises also the study of time-dependent mesoscopic transport. We present now the main results for the
time-dependent current
flowing from noninteracting leads to a central interacting region - as completion of the Meir-Wingreen current formula derivation.

In general, the wavefunction coherence phase can be modified by either external magnetic field or temperature variation. In case an external time-dependence perturbation is applied, the wave function acquires an extra phase factor
exp(-i\int dt^{\prime}\epsilon(t^{\prime}))
. However, the electronic distribution for a given state does not vary applying such a perturbation. Under these conditions, the derivation of the time-dependent current is not conceptually different from the steady-state current we developed so far in this appendix - since the time-dependent feature just appears in the explicit time-parameter definition in the model. Nevertheless, some extensions are required
[
29
]
, as we aim to briefly present in this section.

The external time-dependent driving force is physically the time-dependent voltage bias. Due to the perturbation, the time-dependent model Hamitonian reads the same as presented in Eq.
A.2
but now with an explicit time-dependence in its single-particle energy states and tunnelling terms i.e.

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}\longrightarrow\epsilon_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}(t)=\epsilon_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}+\delta_{\alpha}(t)\penalty\ ,

(A.30)

\displaystyle c^{\dagger}_{\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}c_{\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}\longrightarrow c^{\dagger}_{\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}(t)c_{\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}(t)\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle H^{U}_{imp}\{d^{\dagger}_{n\sigma};d_{n\sigma}\}\longrightarrow H^{U}_{imp}\{d^{\dagger}_{n\sigma}(t);d_{n\sigma}(t)\}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle V_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}\longrightarrow V_{\alpha\mathbf{k}}(t)\penalty\ .

We note the single-particle energy of the uncoupled lead is split into static and time-dependent components, such that under the limit
\delta_{\alpha}(t)\rightarrow 0
, the energy level coincides with its time-independent one. The corresponding time-dependent nonequilibrium leads Green’s function of the isolated model are now defined as

\displaystyle G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{<}(t,t^{\prime})=if^{\mu_{\alpha}}(\omega-\mu_{\alpha})exp\bigg[-i\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}dt_{1}\omega(t_{1})\bigg]\penalty\ ,

(A.31)

\displaystyle G_{0,\alpha\mathbf{k}\alpha\mathbf{k}\sigma}^{R/A}(t,t^{\prime})=\mp\theta(\pm t\mp t^{\prime})exp\bigg[-i\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}dt_{1}\omega(t_{1})\bigg]\penalty\ .

By comparing those expressions to their time-independent counterpart and Fourier transformed ones in Eq.
A.22
, we note now that
(i)
the Green’s functions depend on each time label separately, hence time-translation invariance is broken,
(ii)
the Fourier transform to energy domain is not applicable any longer and the final electrical conductance formula is in time domain,
(iii)
the electronic distribution for a given
\alpha,k
state is not modified, hence we can still use the equilibrium Fermi distribution also in time-dependent transport phenomena.

We introduce now the
time-dependent levelwidth function
valid for
\forall\penalty\ n,m

[\mathbb{\Gamma}^{\alpha}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}},t_{1},t)]_{nm}=\pi\mathbf{V}_{\alpha\mathbf{k}n}\mathbb{V}^{\star}_{\alpha\mathbf{k}m}\rho_{0,\alpha\sigma}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}})exp\bigg[i\int_{t_{1}}^{t}dt_{2}\delta_{\alpha}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}},t_{2})\bigg]\penalty\ ,

(A.32)

that incorporates now the time-dependent part of the lead energy level, compare it with its steady-state version in Eq.
A.25
.

The
general time-dependent Meir-Wingreen current formula
with respect to the left lead reads

\displaystyle\hat{I}_{L}(t)=-2e\sum_{nm\sigma}\int_{-\infty}^{t}dt_{1}\int\frac{d\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}}{2\pi}\mathit{Im}Tr\Big(

\displaystyle e^{-i\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}(t_{1}-t)}[\Gamma^{L}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}},t_{1},t)]_{nm}G^{<}_{nm\sigma}(t,t_{1})+

(A.33)

\displaystyle+f^{\mu_{L}}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}}-\mu_{L})G^{R}_{nm\sigma}(t,t_{1})]\Big)\penalty\ ,

as presented in later work by Meir and Wingreen
[
246
]
. An analogous current
\hat{I}_{R}(t)
expression with respect to the right lead can be derived. However, due to the time-dependence in the transport process,
\hat{I}_{L}(t),\hat{I}_{R}(t)
do not have the same form. As observed in the steady state case, the current expression is fully determined by the local Green’s function of the central region. It is straightforward to reduce the time-dependent result to the time-independent case by switching off the time varying component in the single-particle energy
\delta_{\alpha}(t)\longrightarrow 0
.

We conclude this discussion by introducing the
generalised proportionate coupling condition
, as straightforward time-dependent extension of Eq.
3.63
, namely

[\mathbb{\Gamma}^{L}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}},t_{1},t)]_{nm}=\gamma[\mathbb{\Gamma}^{R}(\epsilon_{\mathbf{k}},t_{1},t)]_{nm}\penalty\ ,

(A.34)

that is valid for
\forall\penalty\ n,m
central-region degrees and it holds only if
\delta_{L}(t)=\delta_{R}(t)\equiv\delta(t)
.

By means of this extension in time domain, it can be derived a
time averaged current
whose form is recognisable as of Landauer-type
[
29
]
. Hence, even in time-dependent interacting transport, by introducing the appropriate constraints, the Meir-Wingreen formula reduces to the Landauer formula in Eq.
3.25
.

We this last discussion we complete an exhaustive derivation on all the aspects involved in the Meir-Wingreen approach. We refer to Sec.
3.3
for the physical implication of this formulation in mesoscopic transport.

Appendix B
Non Equilibrium perturbation expansion of interacting
\Sigma^{</>}

In the section
4.2
on alternative formulation of the Meir-Wingreen current equation, we propose two different approaches. In this appendix we focus on the perturbative one, where we take as prototype the Ng ansatz in Eq.
4.38
and we develop perturbative calculations to derive a corrected Fermi distribution exact up to order of
\mathcal{O}(V^{2}_{bias},U^{4})
for interacting models out-of-equilibrium condition - see Eq.
4.59
. The key idea behind this strategy is to systematically separate the equilibrium from the nonequilibrium contributions such that an explicit voltage bias dependence is found in the expression, see the equation structure in Eq.
4.39
.

In the main text in Sec.
4.2
, we describe the analytical steps required to derive expression for the
\Sigma^{<}_{dd\sigma}
. In this appendix, we present the greater components which share the same formal structure with the lesser ones - just with inverted time variables if we consider their initial location on the Keldysh contour. Further literature references are found in
[
23
,
22
,
28
,
143
]

We adopt the same notation as in the main text and we report it here for convenience. The noninteracting spectral function is calculated for the impurity Green’s function in Eq.
2.27
in a infinite wide-band-limit approximation, see Eq.
3.13
so we use the notation

\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\sigma}(\omega)\doteq\frac{2\Gamma}{(\omega-\epsilon_{d\sigma})^{2}+\Gamma^{2}}\penalty\ .

(B.1)

As derived in the main text, the linear expansion of the Fermi distribution reads

f^{\mu_{L}/\mu_{R}}\big(\omega\mp\frac{eV_{bias}}{2}\big)=f^{Eq}(\omega)+\zeta_{\alpha}\frac{eV_{bias}}{2}c(\omega,T)+\mathcal{O}(V_{bias}^{2})\penalty\ ,

(B.2)

where we used the notation
\zeta_{\alpha}=\pm 1
to indicate the expansion calculated with respect to left or right lead respectively and we define the function

\frac{1}{k_{B}T}\Big(f^{Eq}(\omega)-(f^{Eq}(\omega))^{2}\Big)=\frac{1}{2k_{B}T\Big(1+\cosh(\omega/k_{B}T)\Big)}=\frac{1}{4k_{B}T}\sech^{2}\Big(\frac{\omega}{2k_{B}T}\Big)\doteq c(\omega,T)\penalty\ .

(B.3)

We go through now to the list of expression in parallel to those in the main text for the lesser components.

The analogue of the lesser component in Eq.
4.53
, the
greater self-energy at zero order
reads

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(0),>}_{0,dd\sigma}(\omega,T,V_{bias})

\displaystyle=\Big(\Sigma^{(0),>}_{0,dd\sigma}(\omega,T)\Big)^{Eq}+\Big(\Sigma^{(0),>}_{0,dd\sigma}(\omega,T,V_{bias})\Big)^{NEq}\penalty\ ,

(B.4)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-2i\sum_{\alpha}\Gamma^{\alpha}(\omega)\big((1-f^{Eq}(\omega))-\zeta_{\alpha}\frac{eV_{bias}}{2}c(\omega,T)\big)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \text{at finite}\penalty\ T\\
-2i\sum_{\alpha}\Gamma^{\alpha}(\omega)\big(\theta(\epsilon_{F}-\omega)-\zeta_{\alpha}\frac{eV_{bias}}{2}\delta(\omega-\epsilon_{F})\big)\penalty\ ,\penalty\ \text{at}\penalty\ T=0\end{cases}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\equiv\Sigma^{(0),>}_{0,dd\sigma}+V_{bias}\Sigma^{\prime(0),>}_{0,dd\sigma}+\mathcal{O}(V^{2}_{bias},U^{4})\penalty\ .

The analogous of the lesser component in Eq.
4.54
, the
greater self-energy at second order
at finite temperature, the equilibrium term reads

\displaystyle\Big(\Sigma^{(2),>}_{0,dd\sigma}(\omega,T)\Big)^{Eq}

\displaystyle=-2\pi iU^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\omega_{1}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\omega_{2}\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\sigma}(\omega_{1})\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\overline{\sigma}}(\omega_{2})\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\overline{\sigma}}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}-\omega)\times

(B.5)

\displaystyle\times\Big((1-f^{Eq}(\omega_{1}))(1-f^{Eq}(\omega_{2}))f^{Eq}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}-\omega)\Big)\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\equiv U^{2}\Sigma^{(2),>}_{0,dd\sigma}+\mathcal{O}(V^{2}_{bias},U^{4})

and the analogous of the lesser component in Eq.
4.55
, the nonequilibrium contribution

\displaystyle\Big(\Sigma^{(2),>}_{0,dd\sigma}(\omega,T,V_{bias})\Big)^{NEq}=

(B.6)

\displaystyle=-2\pi iU^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\omega_{1}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\omega_{2}\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\sigma}(\omega_{1})\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\overline{\sigma}}(\omega_{2})\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\overline{\sigma}}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}-\omega)\times

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\alpha}\zeta_{\alpha}\frac{eV_{bias}}{2}\bigg((1-f^{Eq}(\omega_{1}))(1-f^{Eq}(\omega_{2}))c(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}-\omega,T)+

\displaystyle-f^{Eq}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}-\omega)\Big((1-f^{Eq}(\omega_{1}))c(\omega_{2},T)+(1-f^{Eq}(\omega_{2}))c(\omega_{1},T)\Big)\bigg)\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\equiv V_{bias}U^{2}\Sigma^{\prime(2),>}_{0,dd\sigma}+\mathcal{O}(V^{2}_{bias},U^{4})\penalty\ .

The analogous of the lesser component in Eq.
4.56
, at zero temperature the equilibrium contribution reads

\displaystyle\Big(\Sigma^{(2),>}_{0,dd\sigma}(\omega,T=0)\Big)^{Eq}

\displaystyle=-2\pi iU^{2}\int_{\epsilon_{F}}^{\omega+\epsilon_{F}}d\omega_{2}\int_{\epsilon_{F}}^{\omega-\omega_{2}+\epsilon_{F}}d\omega_{1}\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\sigma}(\omega_{1})\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\overline{\sigma}}(\omega_{2})\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\overline{\sigma}}(\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}-\omega)\penalty\ ,

(B.7)

\displaystyle\equiv U^{2}\Sigma^{(2),>}_{0,dd\sigma}+\mathcal{O}(V^{2}_{bias},U^{4})

and the analogous of the lesser component in Eq.
4.57
, the nonequilibrium contribution

\displaystyle\Big(\Sigma^{(2),>}_{0,dd\sigma}(\omega,T=0,V_{bias})\Big)^{NEq}=

(B.8)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha}\zeta_{\alpha}\frac{eV_{bias}}{2}(2\pi)^{2}iU^{2}\bigg(\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\overline{\sigma}}(\epsilon_{F})\int_{\epsilon_{F}}^{\omega}d\omega_{1}\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\sigma}(\omega_{1})\bigg(\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\overline{\sigma}}(\omega_{1}+\epsilon_{F}-\omega)-\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\overline{\sigma}}(-\omega_{1}+\epsilon_{F}+\omega)\bigg)+

\displaystyle+\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\sigma}(\epsilon_{F})\int_{\epsilon_{F}}^{\omega}d\omega_{2}\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\overline{\sigma}}(\omega_{2})\mathcal{A}_{0,dd\overline{\sigma}}(\epsilon_{F}+\omega_{2}-\omega)\bigg)\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\equiv V_{bias}U^{2}\Sigma^{\prime(2),<}_{0,dd\sigma}+\mathcal{O}(V^{2}_{bias},U^{4})\penalty\ .

We collect these findings to give a quantitative version of the Eq.
4.39
, now for the greater component

\displaystyle\Sigma^{>}_{dd\sigma}(\omega,T,V_{bias})=

(B.9)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\Sigma^{(0),>}_{dd\sigma}(\omega,T)+U^{2}\Sigma^{(2),>}_{dd\sigma}(\omega,T)}_{\Sigma^{Eq,>}_{dd\sigma}(\omega,T)}+\underbrace{V_{bias}\Big(\Sigma^{\prime(0),>}_{dd\sigma}(\omega,T)+U^{2}\Sigma^{\prime(2),>}_{dd\sigma}(\omega,T)\Big)}_{\Sigma^{NEq,>}_{dd\sigma}(\omega,T,V_{bias})}+\mathcal{O}(V_{bias}^{2},U^{4})\penalty\ ,

where at finite temperature, the equilibrium elements are given in Eqs.
B.4
,
B.5
and the nonequilibrium elements are given in Eqs.
B.4
,
B.6
. At zero temperature, in Eqs.
B.4
,
B.7
and in Eqs.
B.4
,
B.8
respectively.

These equations for the greater components, together with the lesser components in the main text, we compute the corrected Fermi distribution that its final form is equal to Eq.
4.59
.

The expressions in Eqs.
B.4
-
B.8
are also employed in the derivation of nonequilibrium interacting
G^{<}_{dd\sigma}
, resulting in Eq.
4.71
. This equation has direct application into the Meir-Wingreen formula and so we have access to the linear response conductance, see discussion in the main text.
